Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we liave taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 
We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain fivm automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attributionTht GoogXt "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liabili^ can be quite severe.

PREFACE. 



So far as the contents of the present volume are concerned, prefatory 
observations may be dispensed with, as anything which the editor deemed 
necessary to be said, in the way of explanation or comment, has been 
embodied in footnotes. With regiird to future publications, however, an 
intimation has to be made of an addition to the series, which will be 
gratifying to those interested in Glasgow history. 

The protocol books originally intended to be published down to the year 

1600 were (1) William Hegait's three volumes embracing the period 1547-76; 

(2) Henry Gibson's three volumes 1555-76 and 1598-1603; (3) Archibald 

Hegait's three volumes 1581-1604; (4) John Ros's volume 1588-9; and (5) 

George Huchesone's volume 1587-1605. It has just been ascertained that 

there is preserved in the General Register House, Edinburgh, a protocol 

book relating to Glasgow properties, embracing the period 1530-9, and thus 

of a date considerably earlier than the oldest in the above list. The protocol 

book referred to is that of Michael Fleming, designated in his docquets 

"clerk in the diocese of Glasgow, notary public by apostolic and imperial 

authority." One or two of Fleming's instniments are printed in Liber CoUegii 

Nostre Dornine; but it is believed that the book itself has hitherto escaped 

the notice of local investigators. Arrangements are now being made with 

the view of obtaining abstracts of Fleming's protocols, and it is hoped that 

these, together with the remainder of William Hegait's protocols, will be 

comprised in the next printed volume. 

R. R. 

Glasgow, December, 1896.

under a charter by William Coii^'ghame of Craiganis

narrates another transaction in which Buchanan was 
concerned, and in regard to which he seems to have

iBy an act of the Scottish Parliament passed in the 
reign of James I. (U'29, o. 18) the privilege "to brek 
seysing" is allo>A'ed to those who possessed a prior 
title, but the mode of procedure is not laid down. In 
the only other instances of annulling sasine, other 
than by decrees of the law courts, which have come 
under the editor's notice, a method similar to that 
narrated in the text was adopted. On 2nd March, 
147d-SO, a burgess of Stirling "broke, cassed and 
auulled" the sasine of a tenement in that burgh, "by 
the breaking of a dish and casting the same with 
earth and stone beyond the bounds of the tenement" 
(Stirling Charters, kc, 1., p. 207). Again, at Peebles 
on SOth April, 1459, "Georg Wilyamson has brokyn 
sessyng with thwa (two) dissis and fyr onder the 



dissis, sayand that sessyng was fals and . . . thair 
auch nan to haf sessyng of that land bot hym self." 
(Peebles Records, p. 131).

that q. James Windigait of Freland, grandfather {avus) of William, resigned 
the lands in favour of q. John Windigait (his son and apparent heir) and 
Elizabetht Stewarde, his spouse, and 1.1.; but John died before he and his 
spouse obtained sasine; and King James V., willing that Elizabeth, the spouse, 
should sustain no loss thereby, gave her a charter, under his great seal, of 
the lands in liferent;- and as it now clearly appears that William Mwre is 
nearest heir of John Windigait, the Queen granted the lands to him by 
charter dated at Perth, 14 May, 1564.^ Sasine given in term of the precept. 
Witnesses: Thomas Huchesoune, burgess of the burgh of Glesgw, Archibald 
Mwre, hammerman {fahro farrario) there, Andrew Walker in Frelande, and 
Robert Liddell, servitor.

1 Master James Hamilton was suhdean in 1554 (see 
Protocol No. 211), and in 1556 and subsequent 3^ears he is 
designated in Beffigtrum Magni Sigilli and elsewhere as 
bishop of Lismore (Argyle) and subdean of Glasgow. 
The subdeans of Glasgow had the parishes of Cadder 
and Monkland as their prebend. Subsequent to the 
Reformation, and after Hamilton ceased to hold office, 
the subdeanery was usually held by the minister 
either of Cadder or Monkland. David Cunynghame, 
minister of Cadder and Monkland from 1572 to 1577 
(when he was made bishop of Aberdeen) succeeded 
Hamilton as subdean. Andrew Polwarth, the next 
minister, had a gift of the subdeanery from King 
James VI., on 17th March, 1578 (Ftuti Eecle«ie, iii.. 
p. 49). In 1665 John Rosse, minister of Old Monkland, 
with consent of the dean and chapter of 01a8gx)w, 
resigned the sulnleanery in favour of Glasgow University 
(MunimeiUa Altne, i., p. S95). The earl of Kilmarnock 
about the same time resigned the rights he had in the 
subdeanery (lb., p. 388), these rights having perhaps 
been originally acquired by his ancestor, Robert, lord 



Boyde, from Patrick Walkinschaw, minister of Monk- 
land, and confirmed by King Charles I. on 17th March, 
1624 (R.M.S., viii., No. 601). Anna, duchess of Hamil. 
ton, had apparently some interest in the subdeanery 
which she resigned to the college, as is shown by crown 
charter of confirmation, dated 5th April, 1664 (lb., pp. 
S90.3X By charter from King Charles 11. , in 1670, the 
subdeanery was united to the patrimony of the ooU^;e, 
and there were bestowed on the Professor of Theology 
the honour and privileges of subdean in the chapter of 
Glasgow (MuniinetUa, i., pp. 401-11). A rental of the 
extensive lands and revenues thus acquired by the 
college will be fonnd in Munimenta, i., p. 397. The 
Provanside and Deanside lands, referred to in the text, 
seem to have belonged to the subdeans from an early 
period, as John Stewart, subdean, disposed of an acre Id 
Deanside in 1434. The portions of these lands vested in 
the crown in 1613 were conferred by King James VI. on 
the community of Glasgow, and incorporated with the 
burgh {Oltugoto Charters, pt. ii., pp. 284-91).

iThe old Green of Glasgow extended eastward to 
the confluence of the Molindinar bum \fiith the Clyde. 
On the eastward tide of the burn lay the lands of 
Liningshaugh, adjoining these was Dassiegreen, and 
northward of the latter Peitbog, mentioned in the text. 
Kinclayth bordered on Peitbog and Dassie Green. All 
these lands have long since been incorporated with the 
new Green. Previous to 1588 Peitbog and Dassiegreen 
belonged to the community, but in that year were 
disposed of by the magistrates and council " for releveing 
of thair necessitie" (Gla^^ow Rec. i. pp. 124-6.) About 
■eventy-flve years later the town council apparently 
recognised the desirability of reacquiring the lands for 
the formation of the new Green; and in 1661 William 
Anderson, merchant in Glasgow, and Marion Mylne, 
his spouse, in consideration of " ane certane sowme of 
monye" paid to them, conveyed to the community 
"that particular piece of land or grund called the 
Peitix)g and Dassy Grein, with all partes and pertinentis 
belonging therto, lyand within the territory of the 
samyne burgh and neir therto, boundit betuix the 



Water of Clyd on south, the lands called Kinclaith and 
wthers on the north and the lands of Linningshauch on 
the west partes." The lands thus acquired were, in 1666, 
set on a nineteen years' lease to Robert Rae, merchant, 
owner of adjoining lands on the east called Provoet- 
haugh. One of the provisions of the tack is interesting 
as showing the uses to which this part of the new Green 
was put by the inhabitants : " With this expres condi* 
tioune and provisione allwayes that it sail be leasume 
to the burgessis, inhabitantis of this burghe, who sail 
tak and fish salmond be thair oobillis in the water 
thairabouts, to dry thair netes on the saidis lands of 
Peitbog as they ar now and hes bein in use, they paying 
to the said Robert Rae or his subtenentes as they have 
bein in use, heirtofore, to pay to Johne Louk, possessor 
of the saids landes." inventory of City Writs, i. b. 37). 
Dass, according to one of the interpretations in Jamie- 
son's Scottish Dictionary, signifies a landing place, and 
Dassiegreen may thus have derived its name on account 
of the salmon fishers using it for beaching their boats.

iSir John Stewart of Minto, by the transaotions 
narrated in the three precedinj; protocols, and in others 
which follow, disposed of portions of the lauds acquired 
by him from the collefi^iate church of St. Mary, as set 
forth in Nos. 743 and 744. Sir John's ancestors, who 
took their territorial desitpnation from Minto, in Rox- 
burghshire, and who also owned lands in Lanarkshire 
and Renfrewshire, were closely connected with Glasgow, 
and took a share in the administration of its municipal 
affairs. John Stewart, " the first provest that was in the 
cite of Glasgow " (»'.s., 1454. Charters, i., p. xxxiv. ; ii., 



p. 43), was brother of Sir William Stewart, who acquired 
Minto in 1429. Sir Thomas Stewart (son of Sir William), 
who acquired Arthurlie estate in Renfrewshire by 
marriage, was provost of Glasgow in 148U; and each of 
his successors in the title, for three generations in the 
direct line, held that offlce at one time or another. Sir 
John Stewart, named in the text (great-grandson of Sir 
Thomas) may not have been provost at the date of the 
protocol, but he seems to have been elected, or perhaps 
re-elected, at Michaelmas following (see Na 892). Sir 
John's son Walter was the first Lord Blantyre.

920. Jonet King and John Burne. 28 April, 1567, 7 a.m. 

Jonet King produced a charter whereby John Stewart of Mynto, knight, 
granted to her in liferent and to John Burne and his heirs, heritably, the

William Hie ait. 
Vol. iii.. fol. 108. 



iThe five merk lands of Polmadie and Corshiil had 
been bestowed upon the collegiate church of Dumbarton 
about the year 1450. Subsequent to the Reformation the 
provost of the collegiate church, with consent of the earl 
of Lennox, the patron, disposed of the lands to Sir John 
Stewart of Minto, by a feu charter, dated 4th Noveml)er, 
1564. In a paper read by the late Mr. A. M. Scott to the 
Glasgow Archnological Society, on 17th January, 18b9 
(Transactions, vol. i. pp. 517-33) the question as to the 
parish in which Corshiil lands were situated was dis- 
cussed. Mr. Scott had not discovered any express 



authority of an early date bearing on the point ; and it 
seems that so late as 1818, in a question of minister's 
stipend, it was judioially held that the lands were in 
Cathcart parish. The protocol, it will be observed, states 
^at they were in the parish of Govan. In 1892, the 
lands of Crossbill and Polmadie were included in those 
portions transferred from Renfrewshire to the county 
of Lanark by order of the Boundary Commissioners, 
under the Local Government (Scotland) Act, 1889. 
The parochial arrangements of the lands were not 
interfered with.

927. Lyoune Greinlels and John Sempile. 12 May, 1567, 11 a.m. 

"Robert Forthik, burgcs of Glasgw, as ciiwtioner and souertie for Patrik 
Forthik, his brother, is bundin and obleist to content and pay to Lyoune 
Greinleis the sowme of ane hundreth merkis to be payit in nowmberit 



duty of four merks was stipulated to be paid by the 
grantees, and it was provided that if the Friars should 
be restored they should refrain possession of the yards, 
but the tenement was to remain with Graham for 
payment of a yearly feu duty of three merks. By the 
charter of kirk-livin(fs obtained from Queen Mary, on 
16th March, 1566-7 (Glasgow Charters, ii. pp. 131-7), the 
place, orchards, and other property of the Blackfriars, 
were conveyed to the matcistrates and community of 
Glasgow, and it was declared that all alienations of such 
property by the friars, since the change of religion, 
should be rescinded and annulled. It was no doubt 
with the view of neutralising the effect, on Graham's 
acquisition, of the latter charter that in the following 
month he procured the crown confirmation noticed in 
the text. Ultimately, however, the grant of 1660 had 
to be relinquished. In 1572-3 the city transferred the 
kirk-livings to the college authorities (Fb. pp. 149-62), 
who thereupon instituted an action before the lords of 



council and session for reduction of Graham's title 
Judgment of the court was pronounced on 7 Jime, 1578, 
setting aside the grant of 1500 ; and on 27 February 
following Graham executed in favour of the ooUege a 
formal surrender of the property, there described a» 
"the great yaird sumtyme pertening to the Freiris 
Predicatouris of Glasgw, with the small yairdis adjacent 
thairto, as the West Freir Yarde, the Colhowse, and 
Closter Knot, the Paradyoe Yairdis uver and neather, 
with the remanent small yardis adjacent thairto." See 
Preface to Munimenta Fratrum Predieatorum (pp. 
Ixiiii-lxvii) and authorities there cited. It appears from 
a complaint by the principal and masters of the coUe^ 
to the privy council, on 5th March, 1570-80, ooncemlng 
fraudulent abstraction of the rents and profits of the 
kirk-livings that the practice of churchmen disposing of 
their benefices had not even then ceased (Privj Councfl 
Register iii. p. 274).

24th April, 1509, the same King confirmed the lands to 
Archibald earl of Ergile and incorporated them into 
the free barony of Rosneth (76. No. 3334). The earliest 
reference in the Ureat Seal Register to Rosneath as an 
island is contained in a confirmation by Queen Mary to 
Gilbert M'Kynne, in August, 1548, of the lands of 
Knokdorre-M'Kynne, which arc described as lying "in 
insula de Rosneith" (76. iv. No. 245). On 2nd December, 
1558, King FYancis and Queen Mary confirmed a charter 
by the earl of Argyle to the Lord of Lome of the lands 
of the island of Rosneith (terra* irunUe de Romuith) 
with castle, mills, &c. (76. No. 1315). The lands "in 
insula de Rosneth" mentioned in the text were con- 
firmed to James Campbell of Ardkinlas and spouse by 
crown charter, dated 24th March, 1572-3 (76. No. 2132).
